(Proposed) Trade Name	Balversa
Pharmacologic Class	Kinase inhibitor
Code name	JNJ-42756493
Applicant	Janssen Research & Development
Formulation(s)	Tablets: 3 mg, 4 mg, and 5 mg
Dosing Regimen	8 mg (two 4 mg tablets) orally once daily, with dose increase to 9 mg (three 3 mg tablets) once daily based on serum phosphate levels and tolerability at 14 to 21 days
Applicant Proposed Indication(s)/Population(s)	(b) (4)
Recommendation on Regulatory Action	Accelerated Approval
Recommended Indication(s)/Population(s) (if applicable)	
For the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma (UC), that has:
susceptible FGFR3 or FGFR2 genetic alterations, and
progressed during or following at least one line of prior platinum-containing chemotherapy, including within 12 months of neoadjuvant or adjuvant platinum-containing chemotherapy.
Select patients for therapy based on an FDA-approved companion diagnostic for BALVERSA

L57: ## 1 Executive Summary
L58: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L59: Erdafitinib (BALVERSA™) is a kinase inhibitor that is a new molecular entity and has not previously received marketing approval for any indication by any regulatory body. Approval of erdafitinib also represents the first approval of a drug that specifically targets genetic alterations in the fibroblast receptor growth factor (FGFR) pathway.
L60: Erdafitinib binds to FGFR1, FGFR2, FGFR3 and FGFR4 and inhibits enzymatic activity based on in vitro data. Erdafitinib also binds to RET, CSF1R, PDGFRA, PDGFRB, FLT4, KIT, and VEGFR2. Erdafitinib inhibited FGFR phosphorylation and signaling and decreased cell viability in cell lines expressing FGFR genetic alterations, including mutations, amplifications, and fusions. Erdafitinib demonstrated antitumor activity in FGFR-expressing cell lines and xenograft models, derived from tumor types including bladder cancer.
L61: The clinical portion of the current application was submitted on September 18, 2018 with the proposed indication of the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma,(b) (4)
L62: The dosing regimen proposed by the Applicant is 8 mg (two 4 mg tablets) orally once daily with a dose increase to 9 mg (three 3 mg tablets) once daily if serum phosphate (PO_4) level is &lt; 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity.
L63: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L64: Substantial evidence of effectiveness for this application is obtained from the single arm, multicenter study BLC2001 that enrolled patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer who had tumors with FGFR3 point mutations (R248C, S249C, G370C, Y373C) or FGFR fusions (FGFR2-BICC1, FGFR2-CASP7, FGFR3-TACC3, FGFR3-BAIAP2L1). The efficacy population for this application is obtained from a cohort of 87 patients from BLC2001 who had all previously been treated with chemotherapy, which was cisplatin or carboplatin in 97% of cases. These patients all received erdafitinib at the proposed dose of 8 mg daily with pharmacodynamically-guided dose increase possible to 9 mg daily based on serum phosphate and tolerability. Of these 87 patients, 24% also previously
L65: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L66: received an anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy. The confirmed objective response rate (ORR) in the 87-patient efficacy population was 32.2% and the duration of response (DoR) was 5.4 months (95% CI: 4.2, 6.9) as determined by the Blinded Independent Review Committee (BIRC). A higher ORR was observed in patients with FGFR3 point mutations (N=64, ORR: 40.6%) than in patients with FGFR3 fusions (N=18, ORR: 11.1%) or in patients with FGFR2 fusions, in whom no responses were seen (N=6)1.
L67: Patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer previously treated with a platinum-containing regimen may potentially be treated with anti PD-1/PDL 1 therapy or with chemotherapy. Pembrolizumab has shown a survival advantage over chemotherapy in this population; however, the ORR observed with pembrolizumab is lower than the ORR observed with erdafitinib and time-to-event endpoints such as overall survival (OS) cannot be assessed in BLC2001 since it is a single arm trial. Responses were also observed in patients treated with erdafitinib who had not previously responded to anti PD-L1/PD1 therapy. Additionally, the ORR in published reports of chemotherapy in patients with metastatic urothelial cancer in the second line is lower than the ORR observed with erdafitinib. For example, the chemotherapy arm in the trial that led to the regular approval pembrolizumab had an ORR of 11%. Single agent chemotherapy is also generally more toxic than erdafitinib.
L68: Recommendation for accelerated approval for this application takes into consideration the recommended approach outlined in the Guidance for Industry- Developing Targeted Therapies in Low-Frequency Molecular Subsets of a Disease. Patient subgroups with FGFR3 point mutations and one class of FGFR3 fusions showed responses. Patients with all enrolled FGFR2 fusions and certain enrolled FGFR3 fusions (FGFR3-TACC3_V3 and FGFR3-BAIAP2L1) did not demonstrate responses; however, the review team concluded that there were too few of these patients enrolled on BLC2001 to definitively demonstrate a lack of efficacy in these rare molecular subsets. Based on totality of information and biological feasibility, including the mechanism of action, results of in-vitro studies and results from study patients with other FGFR alterations, the indication statement will include patients with all enrolled FGFR3 and FGFR2 fusions in addition to the more common FGFR3 point mutations. BCL3001 is an ongoing randomized controlled trial of erdafitinib versus chemotherapy or immunotherapy in patients with urothelial cancer whose tumors harbor the same FGFR3 and FGFR2 gene alterations that were eligible for enrolment on BLC2001, with OS as the primary endpoint. Submission of BCL3001 results will be a postmarketing requirement (PMR) as a condition of approval for erdafitinib.
L69: The Applicant presented real-world evidence to support the claim that patients with urothelial cancer and FGFR genetic alterations represents a unique subset of patients of patients who would not be expected to respond as well vs. unselected patients to anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy, one of which (pembrolizumab) has regular approval in this setting based on a prolongation in overall survival.
L70: 1 One patient with a FGFR2-CASP7/FGFR3-TACC3_V3 fusion is reported in both FGFR2 fusion and FGFR3 fusion numbers above
L71: Review of the presented real-world data was inconclusive in establishing this class of patients as having a different expected response to these agents due to issues with methodology. Further, post hoc analysis of responses to prior anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy in BLC2001 also showed a low response rate to previous anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy (4.8%; 1 of 21) compared to responses to prior platinum-based chemotherapy (34.5%, 29 of 84). In addition, a higher response rate (59%) to erdafitinib was observed in patients who received prior the anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy. These observations supports the claim that patients with advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer and FGFR genetic alterations represent a molecularly-defined subset in this setting. The Applicant presented real-world evidence to support the claim that patients with urothelial cancer and FGFR genetic alterations represents a unique subset of patients of patients who would not be expected to respond as well vs. unselected patients to anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy, one of which (pembrolizumab) has regular approval in this setting based on a prolongation in overall survival. Review of the presented real-world data was inconclusive in establishing this class of patients as having a different expected response to these agents due to issues with methodology. Further, post hoc analysis of responses to prior anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy in BLC2001 also showed a low response rate to previous anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy (4.8%; 1 of 21) compared to responses to prior platinum-based chemotherapy (34.5%, 29 of 84). In contrast addition, a higher response rate (59%) to erdafitinib was observed in patients who received prior the anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy. These observations supports the claim that patients with advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer and FGFR genetic alterations represent a molecularly-defined subset in this setting.
L72: The final agreed upon indication for erdafitinib is for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma that has
L73: - susceptible FGFR3 or FGFR2 genetic alterations and
- progressed during or following at least one line of prior platinum-containing chemotherapy including within 12 months of neoadjuvant or adjuvant platinum-containing chemotherapy.
L74: Accelerated approval of erdafitinib is recommended by all disciplines.
L75: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L76: ## **Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment**
L77: Patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer have a poor prognosis despite the high response rate seen with standard platinum/gemcitabine chemotherapy. While pembrolizumab has received regular approval in the second-line setting, patients whose tumors harbor FGFR alterations represent a molecularly-defined subset of patients with urothelial carcinoma. This subset may have a lower response rate to PD-L1/PD-1 inhibitors and represents a population of patients for which an unmet medical need exists.
L78: Erdafitinib is a breakthrough-therapy designated kinase inhibitor targeting susceptible FGFR genetic alterations, that is under review for accelerated approval. Erdafitinib was evaluated in the single-arm phase 2 study BLC2001 which enrolled patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer whose tumors were found to harbor specific FGFR3 and FGFR2 alterations. Efficacy and safety for the labeled indication was assessed via independently-determined confirmed response rate and reported adverse events in a cohort of 87 patients in BLC2001 who received prior chemotherapy, 97% of which was platinum-based. These patients received erdafitinib at a starting dose 8mg with a dose increase to 9mg based on serum phosphate levels and tolerability, which is the proposed dose for this approval.
L79: The BICR-assessed response rate was 32% with a median duration of 5.4 months. Responses appeared to be higher in those patients with FGFR3 mutations (n=64, ORR=40.6%), compared to those with FGFR3 fusions (n=17, ORR=11.1%) and FGFR2 fusions (n=6, ORR 0%). A companion diagnostic will be approved with erdafitinib.
L80: The safety profile of erdafitinib is acceptable in this setting. Deaths due to an adverse event occurred in 1.1% of patients while permanent treatment discontinuation occurred in 12.7%. Grade 3-4 adverse events occurred in 59% of patients and the most common (&gt; 15%) all grade adverse events were hyperphosphatemia, stomatitis, dry mouth, decreased appetite, dry skin, alopecia, palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia syndrome, dry eye, onycholysis, paronychia, nail dystrophy, diarrhea, constipation, nausea, fatigue, urinary tract infection, dysgeusia, musculoskeletal pain, weight decreased, and anemia. Ocular toxicity, hyperphosphatemia, and embryo-fetal toxicity are labeled as warnings and precautions for erdafitinib.
L81: Accelerated approval of erdafitinib is recommended by all disciplines. The Applicant will be asked to submit the results of the randomized phase 3 trial BLC3001 that is underway to confirm the clinical benefit of erdafitinib.
L82: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L83: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer have a poor prognosis despite the high response rate seen with standard platinum/gemcitabine chemotherapy. Patients with tumors that are positive for FGFR genetic alterations appear to represent a molecularly-defined population with uniquely targetable disease.
Patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer who have received prior platinum-based therapy have a serious and life-threatening condition.
Current Treatment Options	Pembrolizumab has received regular approval in this setting for an unselected population based on demonstrating an overall survival advantage over chemotherapy. Atezolizumab, avelumab, durvalumab, and nivolumab have also received accelerated approval for the treatment of patients with urothelial cancer who have received prior platinum-based therapy. Taxane-based chemotherapy, both single agent and combination, is also used in this setting. None of these treatment options specifically target the FGFR pathway.
Pembrolizumab has demonstrated an OS advantage over chemotherapy in this setting. It is not known if patients with advanced or metastatic urothelial cancer and FGFR alterations would be expected to benefit from pembrolizumab to the same extent as unselected patients.
Benefit	In a single-arm trial, the confirmed response rate by Blinded Independent Review Committee for erdafitinib in patients at the proposed dose and line of therapy was 32.2% (95% CI 22.4, 42.0). This included 2 patients with complete responses (2.3%). The median duration of response was 5.4 months (95% CI 4.21, 6.93).
Erdafitinib has demonstrated a good response rate with a median duration of response of approximately half a year and an acceptable safety profile.
Risk and Risk Management	No REMS will be required.
The most commonly reported treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were hyperphosphatemia (75.9%), stomatitis (56.3%), fatigue (54.0%), diarrhea (47.1%), dry mouth (44.8%), and onycholysis (41.4%). Ocular toxicity, hyperphosphatemia, and embryo-fetal toxicity are labeled
The risk-benefit profile of erdafitinib is acceptable in the approved patient population. Clinical trial BLC3001 is ongoing and will provide further data on the efficacy and safety of

L90: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L91: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L92: Urothelial carcinoma is the most common malignancy in the urinary tract system and accounts for approximately 17,240 deaths in 20182,3. Although most urothelial carcinomas are non-muscle invasive at diagnosis and can be managed effectively with surgical resection and/or intravesical therapies, approximately 10-15% of patients may develop invasive, locally advanced, and metastatic urothelial carcinoma4. In addition, approximately 10% of patients have locally advanced or metastatic disease at diagnosis. Standard of care for the first line treatment of patients with advanced disease is platinum-containing combination chemotherapy, such as gemcitabine and cisplatin (GC) or methotrexate, vinblastine, doxorubicin, and cisplatin (MVAC). However, almost all patients experience disease progression or intolerance to treatment during or after platinum-containing chemotherapy.
L93: FGFR genetic alterations occur in about 20% of patients with relapsed or refractory urothelial cancer5. The most common alterations in this setting are FGFR point mutations, specifically point mutations in FGFR3. FGFR fusions (of FGFR3 and FGFR2) are less common in this setting, estimated to be found at a rate of ~5% in advanced urothelial cancer overall per literature and per numbers of patients screened and enrolled in BCL2001. FGFR2 fusions specifically are very rare in bladder cancer, and it is unclear if they represent molecular “drivers” in this setting, although they appear to be of clinical importance in other settings such as hepatobiliary cancers. There are no approved targeted therapies against FGFR for this molecularly-defined subset of urothelial patients.
L94: Patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma which has susceptible FGFR3 or FGFR2 genetic alterations and which has progressed during or following at least one line of prior chemotherapy are incurable. There is an unmet need for effective therapy to treat patients with this serious and life-threatening disease.
L95: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L96: 2 Siegel RL et al. Cancer Statistics 2016. CA Cancer J Clin 2016; 66:7-30.
L97: 3 NCCN Guidelines v1 2016: Bladder Cancer: Bladder Cancer.
L98: 4 Park JC et al. Multimodal management of muscle invasive bladder cancer. Curr Probl Cancer 2014; 38:80-108.
L99: 5 Gust KM, McConkey DJ, Awrey S, et al. Fibroblast growth factor receptor 3 is a rational therapeutic target in bladder cancer. Mol Cancer Ther. 2013;12(7):1245-54.
L100: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L101: In the U.S., pembrolizumab has received regular approval for the treatment of locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma, refractory to platinum-based chemotherapy, based on an overall survival advantage demonstrated in clinical trials (10.3 months vs. 7.4, HR of 0.73; 95% CI: 0.59, 0.91, p=0.004). Other anti-PD-L1/PD1 therapies (i.e., atezolizumab, avelumab, durvalumab, and nivolumab) have received accelerated approval in the second-line setting under the provisions in 21 CFR Part 601 Subpart E (accelerated approval) and thus they do not constitute available therapy with regards to the current application. Several cytotoxic chemotherapeutics may also be used in this setting although they are not specifically approved in the U.S. for this indication and they are generally associated with low response rates and considerable toxicity. Except for vinflunine, response durations remain unknown or unreported. Nab-paclitaxel monotherapy was associated with a response rate of 28% and a longer survival time relatively to other agents. However, the results may not be reliable given the small sample size of a single-arm study. In addition, the heterogeneity of study patient populations is an issue among these studies, which may contribute to the varying response rates and survival times.
L102: Although pembrolizumab has demonstrated a survival advantage in this setting, it is unclear if anti PD-L1/PD-1 therapy in patients with FGFR-altered urothelial cancers has the same benefit as with those with unaltered cancers, as this specific subset was not prospectively identified in the registrational trials.

L128: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L129: ## Assessment of Impurities
L130: - **Drug Substance:** The impurity that exceeded the qualification threshold of (b) (4) % (b) (4) was qualified by nonclinical data submitted by the Applicant (13-week repeat dose studies in rats and dogs; genotoxicity studies and analysis through in silico assessment of structure-activity relationships).
- **Drug Product:** The applicant proposed the following specification limits for three specified degradants (b) (4)(b) (4) in drug product - At a maximum daily clinical dose of 9 mg erdafitinib, these limits translate to a maximum daily dose of (b) (4) \mug, respectively. As such, the individual specifications are below the ICHQ3B qualification threshold (b) (4) \mug TDI whichever is lower).
- (b) (4) in the drug product - These limits translate to a maximum daily dose of (b) (4) \mug. As such, the individual specification is (b) (4) the ICHQ3B qualification threshold. To evaluate the potential toxicity of the impurity in question (b) (4) the Applicant conducted a 1-month repeated dose oral toxicity study in the rats (impurity evaluation; Study # TOX10565). (b) (4) was administered spiked at (b) (4) with impurity (b) (4) and compared to a group that received the same reference batch without the impurity. No adverse findings were reported following treatment with (b) (4) or the test article spiked with the impurity (b) (4). No adverse differences were reported between the two groups. As such, spiking with (b) (4) did not result in additional toxicity. As such, the impurity specification that exceeded the qualification threshold of (b) (4) was qualified by these nonclinical data.

L133: ## 4.4. Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L134: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L135: Premarket Approval Application P180043 for therascreen® FGFR RGQ RT-PCR Kit was submitted to CDRH for evaluation as a companion diagnostic along with this NDA submission to CDER. The assay is a reverse transcription, real-time PCR test for the qualitative detection of four point mutations [p.R248C (c.742C&gt;T, FGFR3 Exon 7), p.S249C (c.746C&gt;G, FGFR3 Exon 7), p.G370C (c.1108G&gt;T, FGFR3 Exon 10) and p.Y373C (c.1118A&gt;G, FGFR3 Exon 10)] and two fusions (FGFR3:TACC3v1 and FGFR3:TACC3v3) in the fibroblast growth factor receptor 3 (FGFR3) gene in RNA samples derived from formalin-fixed paraffin-embedded (FFPE) urothelial tumor tissue. The test is indicated for use as an aid in identifying patients with cases of urothelial cancer (UC) which harbor these alterations and are therefore eligible for treatment with BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib).
L136: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L137: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L138: **Primary Pharmacology:** Erdafitinib (JNJ-42756493, BALVERSA) is an oral fibroblast growth factor receptor (FGFR) tyrosine kinase inhibitor being developed for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma whose tumors have particular FGFR2 and FGFR3 genetic alterations. In an enzymatic assay, erdafitinib inhibited the tyrosine kinase activity of FGFR1-4 with an IC_{50} of 1.2, 2.5, 3, 5.7 nM, respectively. The binding affinity of erdafitinib was tested in a panel of 451 kinases; erdafitinib bound to all 4 members of the FGFR family (1-4) at a K_d of 0.24, 1.1, 1.4, and 2.2 nM, respectively, and to other kinases with less than or similar affinity. In the in vitro binding assay, erdafitinib also bound to RET, CSF1R, PDGFRA, PDGFRB, FLT4, KIT, and VEGFR2 at clinically relevant concentrations. Erdafitinib inhibited growth of BaF3 cells expressing FGFR1, 3 or 4 (IC_{50} of 11 to 24 nM), however showed considerably weaker inhibition against BaF3 cells expressing VEGFR2 and other kinases (IC_{50} of 1190 to 8560 nM). Additionally, erdafitinib demonstrated antiproliferative activity in selected cancer cell lines from various tissues, including bladder cells, with FGFR1-4 alterations (amplification, mutation or fusion) at IC_{50} values ranging from 0.1 to 129.2 nM. Erdafitinib inhibited autophosphorylation of FGFR proteins in cancer cell lines displaying activated FGFR1, 2, 3 and 4 and phosphorylation of key downstream signaling components of the FGFR signaling pathway in NCI-H1581 cells (i.e. pFRS2 -Tyr436, PLC\gamma 1, ERK 1/2, and pS6K). Similarly, erdafitinib demonstrated antiproliferative activity (IC_{50} &lt; 1 nM) in RK3E (rat kidney epithelial) cells that were transfected with constructs for FGFR2 (BICC1, AFF3, CASP7, CCDC6, OFD1) and FGFR3 (TACC3v1, TACC3v3, BAIP2L1) fusion genes and was shown to inhibit FGFR phosphorylation and phosphorylation of key downstream FGFR signaling pathway components (MAPK and S6). In vivo, erdafitinib demonstrated antitumor activity in immune-deficient rodents bearing tumors derived from multiple human cancer cell lines with FGFR1, 2 and 3 alterations (fusion or amplification) including the bladder, lung, GI and liver. Additionally, in a mouse study using patient-derived xenografts (PDX) originated from lung tumors expressing FGFR2 and FGFR3 fusion proteins, antitumor activity was associated with expression of the fusion protein in the tumor.
L139: The in vitro and in vivo data submitted by the Applicant demonstrated that erdafitinib has antiproliferation activity in cell lines expressing FGFR with genetic alterations (i.e. mutation, fusion or amplification) and inhibits downstream signaling pathway components that play a role in cell proliferation and survival. The approved Established Pharmacologic Class (EPC) of "kinase inhibitor" is applicable to erdafitinib based on its pharmacologic activity.
L140: **Safety Pharmacology:** The effect of erdafitinib on nervous system function was evaluated using the Modified Irwin's method. Erdafitinib resulted in a higher incidence of impaired wire maneuver, flaccid body tone and clinical observations of hypotonia and paralysis in male rats at doses \ge 8 mg/kg. The effects of erdafitinib on cardiovascular and respiratory systems were
L141: evaluated in in vitro and in vivo studies. In vitro, results from the hERG (IC_{50} 408 nM [or 182 ng/mL]) and ventricular wedge assays demonstrated that erdafitinib has potential for producing QT prolongation and arrhythmia at concentrations as low as 0.1 \muM (or 44.7 ng/mL). In vivo, erdafitinib, following a single intravenous dose or oral administration to anesthetized or conscious dogs, resulted in a decreased mean heart rate and increased QTc interval, blood pressure and respiration. Additionally, idioventricular rhythm and ventricular escape rhythms were reported. The C_{max} values after single dose were less than human exposure at clinical dose of 8 mg (1399 ng/mL). Erdafitinib had no adverse effects on cardiac repolarization or other electrocardiographic parameters in patients.
L142: **ADME Studies:** Irrespective of dosing regimen (daily vs intermittent dosing), systemic exposure to erdafitinib (C_{max} and AUC) following repeat oral dosing increased in a greater than dose proportional manner with accumulation in rats and dogs in the 13-week studies. Systemic exposure was greater in female rats and male dogs in the 13-week studies. Following a single oral dose of [^{14}C]erdafitinib to male pigmented rats, radioactivity was distributed throughout the body rapidly (0.5 or 1 hours post dose) in the majority of the tissues/organs. By 24/72 hours post dose, radioactivity was either close to or below the limit of quantification in the majority of the tissues, except the eye (T_{1/2} 182.5 hours) and lacrimal gland (T_{1/2} 135.7 hours). Erdafitinib was highly bound to human plasma proteins compared to animals and extensively bound to human \alpha 1-acid glycoprotein (\alpha 1-AGP). Human metabolites were detected in at least 1 species used in erdafitinib safety studies, with the exception of the minor fecal metabolites M27 and M11 (&lt;2%). Oxidative metabolism (O-demethylation of erdafitinib, M6) is the major metabolic pathway in human and rat. Erdafitinib was the major circulating drug-related material in human plasma; there were no circulating metabolites detected. In human and dogs, the majority of drug-related material was excreted in feces, while in rats the majority of drug-related material was excreted by both the biliary and fecal route. Erdafitinib was considered a weak inducer of CYP3A4 and did not act as a reversible CYP inhibitor in human liver microsomes.
L143: **Repeat Dose Toxicology Studies:** The Applicant conducted oral repeat-dose general toxicology studies (up to 13 weeks) of erdafitinib in rats and dogs. Exposures following repeat dose at the highest dose levels in rats (32 mg/kg [human equivalent dose HED= 311 mg/day], AUC 1910 ng\cdoth/mL) and dogs (1.5 mg/kg, [HED= 48.6 mg/day], AUC 1300 ng\cdoth/mL) were much lower than the AUC of 29268 ng\cdoth/mL in humans at the clinical dose of 8 mg. In the repeat-dose studies, the toxicity profile of erdafitinib in the rat and dog showed considerable overlap; however, some findings appeared to be restricted to the rat. In the rat, mortalities were reported following daily dosing at 8 mg/kg/day (HED=77.8 mg/day; 0.003 times the estimated human AUC at the clinical dose of 8 mg) and following intermittent dosing (7 days on/7 days off) at 32 mg/kg (HED=311 mg/day; 0.065 times the estimated human AUC at the clinical dose of 8 mg). The death at 32 mg/kg was attributed to the mineralization of several tissues (heart, aorta and lungs), and cause of death at 8 mg/kg/day was not determined. Clinical observations included limping, paralysis of the hind legs, hunched back, dyspnea, salivation, thin and weak appearance. The predominant microscopic findings reported in rats included atrophy of gland
L144: structures (mammary gland), soft tissue mineralization of multiple organs (kidneys, lung, stomach, tongue, duodenum), decreased cellularity (bone marrow femur), chondroid dysplasia (larynx, trachea), degeneration of odontoblasts (teeth) and degeneration/inflammation heart. The predominant toxicities in both species included soft tissue mineralization of multiple organs (aorta and heart), eye-related findings (corneal atrophy [rats], atrophy of lacrimal gland [rat and dog], and ocular discharge, corneal opacities, thickened corneal endothelium and low tear production [dogs]), epithelial structures (tongue and oral mucosa atrophy, skin-redness crust, thin hair, and nails discolored/broken), atrophy of parotid salivary gland, chondroid dysplasia/metaplasia and abnormal dentin in teeth and degeneration of odontoblasts in rats (teeth). Tooth changes, unlikely to be of concern in an adult human population due to species difference in tooth growth pattern, may be relevant for a pediatric population. In addition, hyperphosphatemia was reported, similar to that reported in patients treated with erdafitinib. Furthermore, biomarker changes indicative of a disturbance in phosphate and vitamin D homeostasis (changes in serum phosphate, 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D3, FGF23, PTH, and calcium), bone turnover (increases in serum ALP and C/N-telopeptides of type I collagen [CTx and NTx]), and in urinary deoxypyridinoline were reported in both species. The microscopic findings reported in the 13-week studies (eye, salivary/harderian gland, tongue, oral mucosa, ovaries and cervix) were not previously reported in the shorter-term toxicology studies in rats and dogs (\le 1 month). In the repeat-dose study in rats, erdafitinib showed effects on female reproductive organs (necrosis of ovaries) at 32 mg/kg, indicating potential impact on fertility in patients. In dogs, decreases in relative prostate (up to 13%) and testes (up to 18%) weights were reported at doses \ge 0.5 mg/kg/day (HED=16 mg/day; 0.058 times the estimated human AUC at the clinical dose of 8 mg).
L145: The animal studies appeared to be predictive for a number of clinical findings. Similar to animals, in patients treated with erdafitinib, dry eye and keratitis (inflammation of cornea) were reported, although the corneal opacities and atrophy found in animals were not noted in patients (possibly due to intervention/use of lubricating drops). Retinal findings (retinal detachment and choriorretinopathy) were also reported in patients but were not reported in animal studies; possibly due to limited retinal evaluation in the general toxicology studies (i.e. limited ocular histopathology, no optical coherence tomography, lack of functional/electroretinography assessment). The findings in the eye can be attributed to the direct effect of erdafitinib on the ocular system, as was evident by the extended presence of erdafitinib ([^{14}C]erdafitinib) in the eye and lacrimal gland in rats (up to t_{1/2} 182.5 hours), effects on the epithelia (target tissue) and pharmacological activity of erdafitinib on FGF/FGFR pathways. In peer-reviewed publications, it has been concluded that fibroblast growth factors (FGFs) and/or fibroblast growth factor receptors (FGFRs) play a key role in lens, corneal and retina
L146: development and adult function in multiple vertebrate species.[1][2][3] FGFR signaling, for example, has been implicated in lens, retina and corneal cell proliferation and differentiation. Furthermore, all four FGFR genes are expressed in the lens, and FGFR1 and FGFR2 are expressed in the retina and cornea. In addition, similar to what was reported in animals, in patients treated with erdafitinib, adverse reactions including onycholysis, paronychia, nail dystrophy, palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia syndrome, alopecia and dry mouth were reported. The chondroid dysplasia/metaplasia reported in both species may be especially relevant for a pediatric population.
L147: **Genetic toxicology:** Using the standard core battery of genetic toxicology studies recommended in ICH guidances, erdafitinib was not shown to be mutagenic, aneugenic or clastogenic.
L148: **Other Toxicology Studies (phototoxicity, skin sensitivity, eye and vascular irritation):** Erdafitinib was not considered phototoxic in an in vivo study in female Long-Evans pigmented rats. Erdafitinib was considered to cause skin sensitization in the murine local lymph node assay at concentrations of \ge 3.5\%. Erdafitinib did not induce any vascular irritation in the HET-CAM assay. In an in vitro Bovine Corneal Opacity-Permeability Eye Irritation assay, erdafitinib induced a severe increase in corneal opacity that was associated with microscopic changes in the corneal epithelium and stroma. As such, erdafitinib scored positive as an eye irritant, which was consistent with in vivo findings reported in the general toxicology studies.
L149: **Reproductive Toxicology:** Erdafitinib was reported to cross the placenta. In an embryo-fetal developmental study in rats, erdafitinib was administered during the period of organogenesis. Erdafitinib at doses \ge 4\text{mg/kg/day} (HED=38.9\text{mg/day}; less than human exposure at the clinical dose of 8 mg) produced teratogenicity (embryo-fetal death, major blood vessel malformations, multiple limb/paw malformations and increased skeletal anomalies), as well as developmental delay at 8\text{mg/kg/day} (incomplete ossification of multiple bones with associated decreased fetal body weight). The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was the highest dose tested of 8\text{mg/kg/day} (HED=77.8\text{mg/day}; 0.0036 times the estimated human AUC at the clinical dose of 8 mg) and for embryo-fetal development was 1\text{mg/kg/day} (HED=9.7\text{mg/day}; less than human exposure at 8 mg). Females of reproductive potential should use effective contraception during treatment and for 1 month after the last dose, which is approximately 5 half-lives (the higher limit of
L150: [1] Rosenthal R., et al., *The fibroblast growth factor receptors, FGFR-1 and FGFR-2, mediate two independent signaling pathways in human retinal pigment epithelial cells*, *Biochemical and Biophysical Research Communications* 337, 2005.
L151: [2] Zhang J. et al., *Fibroblast Growth Factor Receptor 2 (FGFR2) Is Required for Corneal Epithelial Cell Proliferation and Differentiation during Embryonic Development*, *Plos one*, DOI:10.1371, January 2015.
L152: [3] Robinson M., *An Essential Role for FGF Receptor Signaling in Lens Development*, *Semin Cell Dev Biol.* 17(6), December 2006.
L153: erdafitinib T_{1/2} from clinical studies was 120.5 hours). Likewise, lactating women should not breastfeed during treatment with erdafitinib and for 1 month after the last dose.
L154: The submitted nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology data support approval of BALVERSA for the proposed indications.
L155: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L156: None.
L157: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L158: ## Primary pharmacology
L159: The Applicant conducted in vitro and in vivo pharmacology studies with JNJ-42756493.
L160: - In an enzymatic kinase assay (time-resolved fluorescence assay), JNJ-42756493 inhibited the tyrosine kinase activities of FGFR1, FGFR2, FGFR3 and FGFR4 at an IC_{50} of 1.2, 2.5, 3 and 5.7 nM, respectively, while demonstrated lower activity for VEGFR2 (IC_{50} 36.8 nM).

L164: - In vitro, JNJ-42756493 was tested against a panel of 451 kinases in a binding assay. JNJ-42756493 bound to all 4 members of the FGFR family at affinities (K_d) ranging between 0.24 to 2.2 nM. In comparison, JNJ-42756493 bound other kinases in the panel (CSF1R, PDGFRA, FLT4, PDGFRB, KIT, VEGFR2, TIE1, FLT1, EPHA1, LCK, LYN, ABL1, EPHB6, BLK, DDR1) at affinities ranging between 3.4 to 45 nM, with the exception of RET (at 0.94 nM). The remaining kinases tested bound at affinities over 45 nM (Study # JAN001-01-p-0001 and, Perera et al, 2017)[4]. The Cmax of unbound erdafitinib measure in patients at the clinical dose of 8 mg is 6.3 nM (2.8 ng/mL), suggesting binding of erdafitinib to several kinases at clinically relevant exposures.

L205: ## Safety Pharmacology
L206: - **Nervous system function (CNS):** The Applicant evaluated the effect of JNJ-42756493 on the CNS function using the Modified Irwin's method (Study #TOX10080). The study was conducted as part of the GLP-compliant 4-week oral repeated-dose toxicity study in Sprague Dawley rats (4, 8, 16 mg/kg/day; or 32 mg/kg twice per week). The study was reviewed in support of IND 117490. At doses ≥8 mg/kg/dose, JNJ-42756493 increased the incidence of impaired wire maneuver 2 hours post-dose compared to vehicle control, and a few rats in the 32 mg/kg dose group exhibited this impairment up to 24h post-dose. Doses ≥16 mg/kg/dose resulted in flaccid body tone after 24h. In addition, males rats in the 16 mg/kg/day dose group showed clinical signs of hypotonia and paralysis in Week 4. The Cmax values after single dose of 8, 16 mg/kg/day and 32 mg/kg were 10, 30 and 257 ng/mL, respectively, and were less than the human exposure at the clinical dose of 8 mg (1399 ng/mL).
- **Cardiovascular and respiratory (single dose):** The effect of JNJ-42756493 on cardiovascular and respiratory functions were evaluated in a stand-alone GLP-compliant study in conscious non-telemetered male beagle dogs at escalating single oral doses of 1, 2.5 and 5 mg/kg (Study #TOX10104). At ≥2.5 mg/kg, mean heart rate was decreased during the first 2 hours post dose, followed by an increase in rate reported up to 6 hours postdose. At 5 mg/kg, an increase in mean QT, blood pressure and respiration were reported up to 6 hours postdose. Increases in QTcB (8% and 14.2%), QTcF (9 to 13%) and in QTcVDW (10.7% and 10.2%) were also reported. The Applicant additionally stated that idioventricular rhythm (three VPCs in a row, approximately 20 min post-dose) and ventricular escape rhythms (two VEBs in a row, approximately 2 to 3 hours post-dose) were noted. The Cmax values after a single dose of 5
L207: mg/kg was 265 ng/mL and was less than the human exposure at the clinical dose of 8 mg/day (1399 ng/mL).

L212: - **Cardiovascular and respiratory (repeat dose):** In the GLP-compliant 3-month repeat-dose toxicology study in male and female dogs at dose \ge 0.5 mg/kg/day, decreases in heart rate were reported in male (up to 28%) and female dogs (up to 21%) in week 13 (Study #TOX10849). No respiratory effects were reported. The C_{max} values were less than the human exposure at the clinical dose of 8 mg (1399 ng/mL). The one-month dog study was reviewed previously under IND 117490. There were no abnormal ECG findings.
- **Arterially perfused rabbit ventricular wedge:** The potential electrophysiological effects of JNJ-42756493 (0.01, 0.1, 1 and 10 \muM) were evaluated in isolated arterially perfused rabbit ventricular wedge preparations to predict arrhythmogenic potential (Study #CoE2912/HRS014 [non-GLP]). JNJ-42756493 resulted in prolonged QT interval, increased rTp-Te and TdP at concentrations \ge 0.1 \muM (44.7 ng/mL). Contractile force of the ventricular wedge was increased at 1 \muM. The data indicated that JNJ-42756493 has the potential to cause arrhythmia at 0.1 \muM (44.7 ng/mL) and greater.

L226: HERG assay: JNJ-42756493 was evaluated in the HERG assay at concentrations ranging from 30 nM to 10 µM, along with a positive control (astemizole) at one concentration (10 nM). Inhibition of HERG-mediated K current in HEK293 cells was reported at JNJ-42756493 concentrations ≥0.03µM, and IC50 was calculated to be 408 nM (182 ng/mL). The IC50 was lower than the human exposure at clinical dose (1399 ng/mL), suggesting a potential interference of JNJ-42756493 in cardiac repolarization in vivo (Study #COE3216-COE3317 [non-GLP]).

L282: #The abundance of metabolites in feces is considered qualitative due to poor recovery of radioactivity after sample processing of pooled feces extracts. Total recovery of radioactivity (relative to the percent of dose recovered in feces homogenates) was approximately 58% (0-24 h) for rat, and 22% (0-24 h) and 44% (24-48 h) for dog.
L283: 4. &amp; 5. The effects of JNJ-42756493 (0.005 to 15 µM) on the expression of cytochrome P450 enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4) were evaluated in cultures of cryopreserved primary human hepatocytes from three different human donors. JNJ-42756493 was not an inducer of CYP1A2 mRNA and CYP2B6 mRNA in human hepatocytes in vitro. However, JNJ-42756493 was considered a weak inducer of CYP3A4 based on increases in mRNA.
L284: 6. The inhibition potential of JNJ-42756493 (0.1 to 100 µM) towards CYP activity was investigated using human liver microsomes. JNJ-42756493 did not inhibit the activity of CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6. JNJ-42756493 exhibited weak inhibition of CYP3A4.

L338: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L339: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L340: **Study title/number:** JNJ-42756493-AAA: 3-month Repeated Dose Oral Toxicity Study in the Rat (Study # TOX10846)
L341: - Mortalities were reported at 8 mg/kg/day (daily dosing) and 32 mg/kg/day (intermittent dosing, 7 days on/7 days off). The cause of death at 8 mg/kg/day was
L342: not determined, and the deaths at 32 mg/kg/day were attributed to soft tissue mineralization of the heart and aorta which were proposed to have contributed to the rat's poor condition.

- The predominant microscopic findings included atrophy of gland structures (exorbital lacrimal gland, mammary gland, salivary gland), chondroid dysplasia in multiple bones (sternum, stifle, tail), chondroid dysplasia (larynx, trachea), soft tissue mineralization of multiple organs (aorta, eye, heart, kidneys, lung, stomach, tongue, duodenum), atrophy (cornea, oral mucosa, tongue), decreased cellularity (bone marrow of femur), degeneration of odontoblasts (teeth), degeneration/inflammation (heart) and increase in hair follicles/reduced size (skin).
- Test article-related effects on female reproductive organs (necrosis of the ovarian corpora lutea and acute inflammation of the cervix) were reported at 32 mg/kg/day.
L343: (b) (4)
L344: Conducting laboratory and location:
L345: GLP compliance: Yes
L346: ## Methods
L347: Dose and frequency of dosing:
L348: - 0, 4 or 8 mg/kg/day (daily dosing Day 0 to 90)
- 16 or 32 mg/kg/day (intermittent dosing, 7 days on/7 days off).
L349: Route of administration: Orally by gavage
L350: Formulation/Vehicle: 20% w/v hydroxypropyl-\beta-cyclodextrin (HP-\beta-CD) in demineralized water, HCl to pH 4 \pm 0.1
L351: Species/Strain: Rat [Sprague-Dawley (Crl: CD®)]
L352: Number/Sex/Group: 10/sex/group (main study)
L353: Age: Approximately 6 weeks
L354: Satellite groups/unique design: 3/sex/group (TK animals)

L382: ## **Gross Pathology**
L383: - At doses \ge4mg/kg/day, deformity and discoloration of bones were reported in males and females.
- At doses \ge8mg/kg, discoloration/malocclusion of the teeth were reported in males and females.
- At 32 mg/kg, firm and discolored aorta and/or discolored hearts were reported in the two moribund sacrificed males.
- At 32 mg/kg, thickened and discolored stomach wall was reported in males.
- At 32 mg/kg, irregular fur was reported in males and females.
- At 32 mg/kg, malformation, swelling and red discoloration of the paw nailbed were reported in males and females.
L384: ## **Organ Weights**
L385: No adverse test article-related findings were reported, except for a decrease in female heart weight at 32 mg/kg.

L398: ## **Study title/ number:** JNJ-42756493-AAA : 3-Month repeated dose oral toxicity study in the beagle dog/ Study # TOX10849
L399: - At doses \ge 0.5 mg/kg/day, ocular changes consisted of bilateral watery or purulent ocular discharge, periocular alopecia, blepharospasm, diffuse corneal opacification and low tear production.
- The predominant target organs were aorta (vascular mineralization/degeneration), heart (mineralization/degeneration), bone femur and sternum (chondroid dysplasia), skin (increased hair follicles) and epithelial atrophy in the tongue, oral mucosa, parotid salivary glands, eye, teeth and lacrimal glands.

L514: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L515: ## Embryo-Fetal Development
L516: **Study title/ number:** JNJ-42756493-AAA: *Embryo-fetal toxicity study by the oral route (gavage) in the rat (Segment II)/AB21601 (Study # tox12329)*
L517: ## Key Study Findings
L518: - JNJ-42756493-AAA resulted in reduced maternal body weight at doses \ge 1 mg/kg/day; however, no effects were reported following adjustment of body weight to gravid uterus weight. The effect was likely due to postimplantation loss.
- JNJ-42756493-AAA was teratogenic (embryo-fetal death and structural anomalies) at doses \ge 4 mg/kg/day.
- Developmental delay was reported at 8 mg/kg/day (incomplete ossification of multiple bones with associated decreased fetal body weight).
- The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 8 mg/kg/day (highest dose tested) and for embryo-fetal development was 1 mg/kg/day.
L519: Conducting laboratory and location:
L520: GLP compliance: Yes
L521: ## Methods
L522: Dose and frequency of dosing: once daily on Gestation Day (GD) 6 to 17 (organogenesis period)
L523: Route of administration: oral gavage
L524: Formulation/Vehicle: 20 % w/v hydroxypropyl-\beta-cyclodextrin
L525: Species/Strain: Sprague-Dawley rats
L526: Number/Sex/Group: 22/pregnant females/group
L527: Satellite groups: toxicokinetics evaluation (dams and fetuses) on GD 6 and 17. On GD 17, blood was collected from pregnant rats and was collected from each fetus.
L528: Study design: doses of 1, 4 or 8 mg/kg/day
L529: ## Observations and Results
L530: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	No unscheduled death was reported
Clinical Signs	No test article-related clinical signs were reported

L558: ## 5.5.5. Other Toxicology Studies
L559: ## Phototoxicity
L560: The Applicant evaluated the effect of JNJ-42756493 on phototoxicity in in vitro and in vivo studies (Study #TOX1235; non-GLP; TOX10684, GLP).
L561: The in vitro phototoxic potential of JNJ-42756493-AAA was assessed using Balb/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts assay. JNJ-42756493-AAA was considered phototoxic in the in vitro study (Study #TOX1235; non-GLP), while in a follow-up GLP-compliant toxicology study in female Long-Evans pigmented rats, JNJ-42756493-AAA (orally 3 qd at 4, 8, and 16 mg/kg) was not considered to have phototoxic effects on the skin. Systemic exposure increased in a dose-dependent manner. AUC values were 10.1, 79.9 and 544 ng.h/mL for 4, 8 and 16 mg/kg/day, respectively. (Study # TOX10684, GLP)
L562: ## Skin Sensitization
L563: A murine local lymph node assay (LLNA) was conducted to assess the skin sensitization potential of topically applied JNJ-42756493-AAA. CBA/Ca mice (4 females/group) were administered JNJ-42756493-AAA at concentrations of 2.5%, 5%, and 10% w/v or a positive control (25% v/v hexyl cinnamic aldehyde [HCA]). Mice were treated (25 µL) daily for 3 days at the dorsal surface of both ears. No dermal irritation of the ears was reported at all doses tested. JNJ-42756493-AAA at concentrations ≥ 5% resulted in stimulation index of ≥4.1 with calculated concentration (EC3) of ≥3.5%. In conclusion, JNJ-42756493-AAA caused skin sensitization at concentrations of ≥ 3.5%. (Study # TOX10363; non-GLP)

L571: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)

L590: ## 6 Clinical Pharmacology
L591: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L592: The Applicant is seeking accelerate approval of BALVERSA for the treatment of adult patients with locally advanced or metastatic urothelial carcinoma, (b) (4)
L593: The proposed dosing regimen is 8 mg (two 4 mg tablets) orally once daily with a dose increase to 9 mg (three 3 mg tablets) once daily if serum phosphate (PO_4) level is &lt; 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity. The primary evidence of efficacy to support the proposed dosing regimen comes from Applicant's pivotal Phase 2 Study BLC2001. An ORR (CR + PR, BIRC-assessed) of 32% was observed in 87 patients in the target population who received erdafitinib treatment at the proposed dosing regimen. The safety evaluation of data from patients showed that erdafitinib at the proposed dosing regimen was managed per protocol, with a dose reduction rate of 52%, dose interruption rate of 62%, and a treatment discontinuation rate of 12%. The most common adverse reactions included hyperphosphatemia, stomatitis, dry mouth, decreased appetite, and dry skin.
L594: The clinical pharmacology review focused on the evaluations of the proposed dosing regimen, food effect, drug-drug interactions, organ impairment, population PK modeling, and exposure-response analyses for safety and efficacy.
L595: ## Recommendations
L596: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information contained in NDA 212018. This NDA is approvable from a Clinical Pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations/comments are summarized below.
L597: REVIEW ISSUE	RECOMMENDATIONS/COMMENTS
Pivotal or supportive evidence of effectiveness	
The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from pivotal Study BLC2001 in patients with urothelial carcinoma with FGFR alterations. An ORR of 32% was observed in 87 patients who received erdafitinib treatment at the proposed dosing regimen.
The safe use of the drug was managed per protocol, with dose reductions (52%) and dose interruptions (62%). The drug discontinuation rate was 12%. The most common adverse reactions included hyperphosphatemia, stomatitis, dry mouth, decreased appetite, and dry skin.

General dosing instructions	
The proposed dosing regimen is 8 mg once daily with dose increase to 9 mg once daily if serum PO_4 level is < 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity. Erdafitinib may be taken without regard to food. Dose was increased to 9 mg in 41% of patients in the pivotal Phase 2 Study BLC2001.
L598: Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	The following dosing recommendations should be followed:Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inhibitors: consider alternative agents or monitor closely for adverse reactions
Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers: avoid use. A PMC trial is requested to determine the dosing strategy.
Moderate CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers: increase BALVERSA dose up to 9 mg
CYP3A substrates: avoid use with sensitive CYP3A substrates with narrow therapeutic index
P-gp substrates: separate dose by \ge 6 h before or after administration of P-gp substrates with narrow therapeutic index
OCT2 substrates: consider alternative agents or reducing the dose OCT2 substrates based on tolerability
Serum phosphate level-altering agents: avoid use with agents that can alter serum phosphate levels before the initial dose increase period based on serum phosphate levels (Days 14 to 21)

Labeling	Overall, the proposed labeling recommendations are acceptable upon the applicant's agreement to the FDA revisions to the label. Clinical pharmacology labeling recommendations are detailed in Section 11.
Initial Bridge between the to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulations	The to-be-marketed tablets (3, 4, 5 mg film-coated tablets) was used in the proposed dosing regimen arm (Regimen 3) in pivotal Study BLC2001 and in most of the clinical pharmacology studies (i.e., no bridging is needed). Early clinical studies used oral solution, capsules, and initial film-coated tablets; the formulations showed similar erdafitinib pharmacokinetics compared to to-be-marketed tablets (see Appendix).
L599: ## Post-Marketing Requirement (PMR) or Commitment (PMC)
L600: The following issues should be addressed as PMRs and PMCs:
L601: ## **PMRs:**
L602: - Complete and submit the results of your ongoing hepatic impairment study EDI1008 entitled "A Phase 1, Open-Label, Single-Dose Study to Evaluate the Effect of Hepatic Impairment on the Pharmacokinetics of Erdafitinib".
- Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to evaluate the effect of repeated doses of erdafitinib on the single dose pharmacokinetics of a sensitive CYP3A substrate (e.g., midazolam), to address the potential for excessive drug toxicity or decreased drug exposure, and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations when coadministering erdafitinib with a sensitive CYP3A substrate.
L603: ## **PMCs**
L604: - Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to evaluate the effect of repeated doses of a strong inducer (e.g., rifampin) of CYP2C9 and CYP3A on the single dose pharmacokinetics of erdafitinib to assess the magnitude of decreased drug exposure and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations when erdafitinib is coadministered with CYP2C9 and CYP3A inducers.
- Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic study to evaluate the effect of repeated doses of erdafitinib (at steady-state) on the single dose pharmacokinetics of a probe
L605: substrate of OCT2 to determine appropriate dosing recommendations for a probe substrate of OCT2 when it is coadministered with erdafitinib.
L606: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L607: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L608: Erdafitinib is a FGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor. The following is a summary of the clinical pharmacokinetics of erdafitinib:
L609: **Absorption:** Median T_{MAX} was 2.5 h (range: 2 to 6 h). No clinically meaningful differences in erdafitinib pharmacokinetics were observed following administration of a high-fat, high-calorie meal in healthy subjects.
L610: **Distribution:** Over 99% protein is bound to human plasma proteins, primarily to alpha-1-acid glycoprotein. The mean apparent volume of distribution was 29 L in patients.
L611: **Metabolism:** Erdafitinib is primarily metabolized by CYP2C9 and CYP3A. The contribution of CYP2C9 and CYP3A in the total clearance of erdafitinib is estimated to be 39% and 20%, respectively.
L612: **Elimination:** The mean total apparent clearance was 0.36 L/h and the mean effective half-life was 59 h in patients.
L613: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L614: ## General Dosing
L615: The recommended dosing regimen is 8 mg once daily with dose increase to 9 mg once daily if serum phosphate (PO_4) level is &lt; 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity.
L616: ## Therapeutic Individualization
L617: Regarding drug-drug interactions (DDIs), the following dose modifications are recommended:
L618: - **Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inhibitors:** consider alternative agents or monitor closely for adverse reactions
- **Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers:** avoid use
- **Moderate or Weak CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers:** increase dose up to 9 mg
- **CYP3A substrates:** avoid use with sensitive CYP3A substrates with narrow therapeutic index
- **P-gp substrates:** separate dose by \ge 6 h before or after administration of P-gp substrates with narrow therapeutic index
- **OCT2 substrates:** consider alternative agents or reducing the dose OCT2 substrates based on tolerability
L619: - **Serum phosphate level-altering agents:** avoid use with agents that can alter serum phosphate levels before the dose increase period based on serum phosphate levels (Days 14 to 21)
L620: ## Outstanding Issues
L621: Two PMRs and two PMCs are issued, as presented in the PMR/PMC Section above. There are no other outstanding issues.

L623: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L624: PHYSICO CHEMICAL PROPERTIES

PHARMACOLOGY
Mechanism of action	Erdafitinib is an oral inhibitor of the pan-fibroblast growth factor receptor (FGFR) tyrosine kinase inhibitor (IC_{50} \approx 0.1 to 129 nM for FGFR1 to FGFR4).
Active moiety	There are no major circulating metabolites. Unchanged erdafitinib was the major drug-related moiety in plasma.
QT/QTc prolongation	Erdafitinib treatment has no large effects (i.e., 20 ms) on QTc interval.
GENERAL INFORMATION
Bioanalytical assay	Validated bioanalytical assays (LC-MS/MS) were developed to measure the concentrations of erdafitinib in the clinical studies included in this NDA (see Appendix).
Patient PK vs. healthy subject (HS) PK	Erdafitinib mean C_{max} and AUC were 48% and 40%, respectively, higher in patients compared to healthy subjects—notably due to \sim2-fold higher alpha-1-acid glycoprotein (AGP) in patients than in healthy subjects.
L625: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L626: Steady-state exposure at the proposed dosing regimen	
Steady-state is achieved within 14 days of QD dosing.
At 9 mg QD at steady-state:
AUC_{0-24h} \approx 2020 \text{ ng}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (41% CV)
C_{MAX} \approx 39600 \text{ ng}/\text{mL} (44% CV)

Minimal effective dose or exposure	Unknown.
Maximum tolerated dose or exposure	Not reached, based on Phase 1 Study EDI1001 in patients with advanced or refractory solid tumors or lymphoma (highest evaluated dose = 12 mg QD).
Dose proportionality	
Erdafitinib demonstrated linear PK in humans. Following multiple-dosing, erdafitinib exposures increased dose proportionally across the dose range of 0.5 to 12 mg once daily (see plots below showing beta slope close to unity). Erdafitinib PK appears to be time-independent, based on similar apparent CL (0.245 – 0.521 L/h) across the dose range of 0.5 to 12 mg once daily from Phase 1 Study EDI1001.

DISTRIBUTION
Volume of distribution (Vd)	Erdafitinib is > 99% bound to human plasma proteins, preferentially to alpha-1-acid glycoprotein. The mean apparent Vd in patients is 29 L (51% CV).
Substrate of transporter systems	Erdafitinib is a substrate of P-gp.
ELIMINATION
Terminal elimination half-life and clearance	The mean effective half-life of erdafitinib in patients is 59 h. The steady-state apparent clearance is approximately 0.36 L/h, with an inter-subject variability of 51%.
Metabolism	Erdafitinib is primarily metabolized by CYP2C9 (39% contribution of total clearance) and CYP3A (20% contribution of total clearance). Due to CYP2C9 polymorphism, patients with
L628: CYP2C9*3/*3 genotype ("worst case" poor metabolizer) are expected to have higher systemic concentrations of erdafitinib.
Excretion	Based on the mass balance study, 69% of the dose is recovered in the feces (19% as unchanged) and 19% of the dose was recovered in the urine (13% as unchanged).
Drug interaction liability	Metabolism:As victim: primarily metabolized by CYP2C9 and CYP3A
As perpetrator: CYP3A inhibitor and inducer
Transporter:As victim: P-gp substrate
As perpetrator: P-gp and OCT2 inhibitor
L629: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L630: ## Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L631: Yes.
L632: Evidence of effectiveness was obtained from Applicant's pivotal Study BLC2001.
L633: Study BLC2001 was a Phase 2, multi-center, open-label study to evaluate multiple dosing regimens of erdafitinib in patients with metastatic or surgically unresectable urothelial cancer with FGFR genetic alterations (N=210). The primary objective was to evaluate ORR (CR + PR) of the selected dosing regimen of erdafitinib. Erdafitinib was administered without regard to food.
L634: The following dosing regimens were evaluated (1 cycle = 28 days):
L635: - Initial randomization (1:2)**Regimen 1 (n=33):** 10 mg QD 7 days on/7 days off with potential dose increase* to 12 mg QD 7 days on/7 days off on Cycle 2, Day 1
- **Regimen 2 (n=78):** 6 mg QD with potential dose increase* to 8 mg QD on Cycle 2, Day 1
- Dose Selection (following interim analysis of Regimen 1 and Regimen 2 data, including population PK/PD modeling)**Regimen 3 (n=87):** 8 mg QD with potential dose increase* to 9 mg QD on Cycle 1, Day 15—*proposed dosing regimen for this NDA*
L636: *Dose increase was based on whether the measured serum PO4 level &lt; 5.5 mg/dL (Regimen 1: measured on Cycle 1, Day 21; Regimen 2: measured on Cycle 2, Day 1; Regimen 3: measured on Cycle 1, Day 14), in addition to consideration of any drug-related toxicity. Treatment with Regimen 3 resulted in an BIRC-assessed ORR of 32% (95% CI: 22%, 42%).
L637: Overall, the clinical pharmacology information was adequate to provide supportive evidence of effectiveness of erdafitinib based on ORR in the intended patient population. The clinical pharmacology program, including the exposure-response analyses (refer to Dose Rationale Section below and Pharmacometrics Review in the Appendix), supported the following:
L638: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L639: - The proposed dosing regimen of 8 mg once daily with dose increase to 9 mg once daily if serum PO4 level is &lt; 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity
- There was a positive exposure-response relationship for safety (higher dose leads to more adverse events)
- Exposure-response analysis for efficacy indicated that 9 mg appears to be at the plateau for efficacy
- Clinical safety profile was manageable with dose reductions (52%) and dose interruptions (62%)
- Exploratory subgroup analyses of limited clinical data suggest heterogeneity in response to erdafitinib according to the FGFR alteration type
- Refer to Clinical Pharmacology Sections below for details regarding PK, food effect, DDIs and effect of FGFR alteration type on response
L640: ## **Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?**
L641: Yes. The Applicant's proposed dosing regimen of 8 mg once daily with dose increase to 9 mg once daily, if serum PO4 level is &lt; 5.5 mg/dL from Days 14 to 21 and there is no drug-related toxicity. The proposed dosing regimen is reasonable and appropriate for the intended patient population for which the indication is being sought. The proposed dosing regimen is supported by the observed efficacy, safety, and PK data from pivotal Study BLC2001, as noted below.
L642: ## **Dose Selection Rationale**
L643: Erdafitinib dose of 8 mg daily is the proposed starting dose with the potential for increasing to 9 mg daily on Day 14 based on whether serum phosphate concentration meet a pre-defined threshold of 5.5 mg/dL. There is only one chance for each patient to increase to the 9 mg daily dose. No further dose increase is allowed but dose interruption is possible based on tolerability. The proposed dosing adjustment schedules is shown in Table 56.
L644: **Table 56: Dose Adjustment Schedule for the Proposed Pharmacodynamically-Guided Dosing Algorithm Based on Regimen 3 (BLC2001)**
L645: Phosphate concentration	< 5.5 mg/dL	5.5 – 7 mg/dL	7 – 9 mg/dL	> 9 mg/dL
Individualized dose titration at Day 14	9 mg QD	8 mg QD	Interruptiona	Interruptiona
Individualized dose titration after Day 14	Same dose	Same dose	Interruptiona
Restart lower doseb if treatment had to be interrupted twice for the same reason; otherwise restart same dose	Interruptiona
Restart lower doseb
L646: a Until PO4 &lt; 5.5 mg/dL
L647: b Dose reduction levels: 8 mg (if up-titrated) – 6 mg – 5 mg – 4 mg – 0 mg
L648: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation (NDA [NME] 212018)
## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)
L649: Erdafitinib dose up-titration from 8 to 9 mg daily is to optimize benefit-risk ratio in individual patients based on their serum phosphate concentrations. Both the continuous and the intermittent schedules were evaluated for safety and efficacy in the Phase 2 study BLC2001, with a 6 mg and 8 mg starting dose with intra-subject up-titration to 8 mg and to 9 mg respectively for the continuous schedule and 10 mg starting dose with intra-subject up-titration to 12 mg for intermittent schedule based on serum phosphate levels.
L650: Serum phosphate concentration is a marker of FGFR target engagement in early cycles of erdafitinib treatment. In Study BLC2001, analysis of anti-tumor response versus serum phosphate concentration in the first 3 months in the 8 mg daily regimen showed that responses were achieved both above and below 5.5 mg/dL, with a small advantage to subjects who achieved serum phosphate concentrations above 5.5 mg/dL.

L655: Simulations reflecting a dose increase based on serum phosphate levels and a serum phosphate-based dose modification algorithm confirmed a higher proportion of subjects achieved target serum phosphate levels (≥ 5.5 mg/dL) with a starting dose of 8 mg once daily than with 6 mg once daily.
L656: The PK/PD modeling was performed to assess different scenarios for dosing, including up-titration to 8 mg from a starting dose of 6 mg, or a starting dose of 8 mg with potential up-titration to 9 mg. Based on the PK/PD modeling, the starting dose of 8 mg daily was selected at the interim analysis, with a provision for up-titration to 9 mg for subjects with serum phosphate levels &lt;5.5 mg/dL on Day 14. Approximately 41% of subjects starting at 8 mg were up-titrated to 9 mg daily dose (Table 1; Mod2.7.3/SCE/Table 28). This approach of intra-subject up-titration based on their individual serum phosphate levels (rather than down-titration from a 9 mg
L657: starting dose) was deemed appropriate to increase the tolerability of erdafitinib and improve the chances of treatment success.
L658: The efficacy results for the individualized up-titration to 9 mg daily dose based on Day 14 serum phosphate concentration showed improvement in the objective response rate (ORR: complete + partial response [CR+PR]) and best overall response compared to 8 mg daily dose. The results for disease control rate (CR+PR+ stable disease [SD]), duration of response, progression free and overall survival (PFS, OS) are comparable for 8 mg and 9 mg daily doses (Table 1).

L666: Exposure-response analysis characterized a significant relationship of higher investigator-assessed ORR, DCR, and PFS with increasing serum phosphate concentrations (Figure 3). The incidence of eye disorders, central serous retinopathy, nail disorders, palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia, and skin disorders, at any grade, had a significant relationship with mean serum phosphate levels up to the day of the adverse event (for details, please refer pharmacometrics review in the Appendix). Treatment with 8 mg erdafitinib once daily was associated with clinically manageable events that were reversible upon temporary treatment
L667: interruption. The totality of the data including the exposure-response analysis support the 8 mg once daily starting dose with potential for a pharmacodynamically-guided individualized up-titration to 9 mg once daily and a stepwise dose reduction and/or interruption for tolerability.

L673: Overall, erdafitinib dose up-titration from 8 to 9 mg daily appears to provide a strategy to optimize benefit-risk ratio in individual patients based on their serum phosphate concentrations. Further increase erdafitinib dose beyond 9 mg daily may have no further benefits based on
L674: *exposure-response analysis for efficacy. And safety of daily dose of 12 mg is a serious concern based on safety data from Phase I study EDI1001. In addition to serum phosphate concentration, FGFR alteration was also a significant predictor for efficacy. Patients with FGFR fusion had much lower response rate than patients with FGFR3 point mutation. See Table 77.*
L675: ## **Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?**
L676: No. Population PK analysis indicated that age (21 – 88 years old), sex, race, body weight (36 – 132 kg) are not significant covariates for erdafitinib clearance. The relevance of the covariate effects was limited (&lt;30%). Furthermore, since erdafitinib dosing is individualized based on phosphate concentrations, dose adjustments based on age, body weight, gender, race are not needed in adult subjects with bladder cancer. In addition, no dose adjustment is proposed in patients with renal or hepatic impairment (see below).
L677: Note that patients with CYP2C9*3/*3 genotypes may have higher erdafitinib plasma concentrations due to reduced metabolic clearance. Monitor for increased adverse reactions in patients who are known or suspected to have CYP2C9*3/*3 genotype.
L678: ## Renal Impairment (RI)
L679: No dose adjustment is needed in patients with RI.
L680: Population PK analysis indicated that there was no clinically meaningful effect of mild (eGFR [estimated glomerular filtration rate, using modification of diet in renal disease equation] 60 – 89 mL/min/1.73 m2, N=156) or moderate (eGFR 30 – 59 mL/min/1.73 m2, N=95) RI on the systemic exposure of erdafitinib. The PK of erdafitinib in patients with severe RI or RI requiring dialysis is unknown due to lack of clinical data.
L681: ## Hepatic Impairment (HI)
L682: No dose adjustment can be recommended in patients with mild HI at this time.
L683: The PK of erdafitinib in subjects with moderate or severe HI is unknown. Population PK analysis indicated that there was no clinically meaningful effect of mild (total bilirubin ≤ ULN and AST &gt; ULN, or total bilirubin &gt; 1.0 – 1.5 x ULN and any AST) HI on the systemic exposure of erdafitinib (n=69, from combined clinical Studies). A clinical study to evaluate single-dose PK of erdafitinib in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe HI, and normal hepatic function is currently ongoing. A PMR is issued to ensure that this study is completed and a final clinical study report is submitted.
L684: ## Tumor FGFR alteration type
L685: To determine molecular eligibility in Study BLC2001, patients were centrally screened for 5 fusions (FGFR2-BICC1, FGFR2-CASP7, FGFR3-TACC3_V1, FGFR3-TACC3_V3, FGFR3-BAIAP2L1) and 4 FGFR3 point mutations (exon 7 [R248C, S249C], exon 10 [G370C, Y373C]) in archival tumor tissue using a RNA-based polymerase chain reaction (PCR) clinical trial assay (CTA). See

## BALVERSA™ (erdafitinib)

Of note, the Blinded Independent Review Committee (BIRC) assessment of disease for this patient was performed in response to a Clinical Information Request of 16 November 2018 leading to a response assessment of progressive disease (PD).

L692: Results of an exploratory multivariate analysis conducted by the reviewer by combining data of patients with either point mutations or fusions enrolled in the 6 mg and 8 mg dosing cohorts showed that FGFR alteration type (point mutation vs fusion) remained independently associated with INV-ORR (P=0.05), considering other factors that may be associated with response (smoking status [never/previous/current], TNM stage at diagnosis [OA-II/III-IV], and dosing cohort [6 mg/8 mg].
L693: ## Exploratory Biomarker analysis – Circulating Tumor DNA
L694: Plasma for circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) was collected in study BCL2001 to explore whether FGFR alterations analyzed using next generation sequencing (NGS) could be detected in blood as a potential replacement for tumor tissue testing in the future. Samples from 155 patients for whom ctDNA was detected at screening (baseline) were included in the analyses (cutoff date of 7 May 2018). The concordance between ctDNA and the tissue-based assay used to determine eligibility in the study was 63.1% for point mutations and 24.1% for fusions; all concordant fusion samples were FGFR3-TACC3 positive. Based on the applicant exploratory analyses, patients with FGFR3 point mutations confirmed by both methods had a higher likelihood of clinical response. Additional FGFR point mutations (e.g., FGFR3 G380R, FGFR2 R664W) were detected in samples of a few patients with the use of NGS.
L695: Reviewer comment: FGFR3 point mutations and fusions are considered important driver events in bladder/urothelial cancer. Data on the pathogenicity of FGFR2 variants in urothelial cancer are sparse. Exploratory subgroup analyses of limited clinical data suggest heterogeneity in response to erdafitinib according to the FGFR alteration type that persists after considering other factors that may influence responsiveness. Whether this heterogeneity reflects a differential sensitivity of FGFR alterations to erdafitinib or is due to small numbers and the exploratory nature of the analyses remains to be determined.
L696: ## Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions (DDIs), and what is the appropriate management strategy?
L697: There are no clinically relevant food-drug interactions—erdafitinib may be taken without regard to food.
L698: There are clinically relevant DDIs—the following dose modifications are recommended (details provided in Sections below):
L699: - **Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inhibitors:** consider alternative agents or monitor closely for adverse reactions
- **Strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers:** avoid use
- **Moderate or Weak CYP2C9 / CYP3A inducers:** increase dose up to 9 mg
- **CYP3A substrates:** avoid use with sensitive CYP3A substrates with narrow therapeutic index
- **P-gp substrates:** separate dose by \ge 6 h before or after administration of P-gp substrates with narrow therapeutic index
- **OCT2 substrates:** consider alternative agents or reducing the dose of OCT2 substrates based on tolerability
- **Serum phosphate level-altering agents:** avoid use with agents that can alter serum phosphate levels before the initial dose increase period based on serum phosphate levels (Days 14 to 21)
L700: ## Food-Drug Interaction
L701: Study EDI1006 was a randomized, open-label, 2-way, cross-over study to evaluate the effect of food on the PK of erdafitinib in healthy subjects (n=16). Subjects were randomized 1:1 into 1 of 2 dosing sequences (fasted/fed or fed/fasted). Subjects were administered a single-dose of 9 mg erdafitinib with or without a high-fat, high-calorie breakfast (washout period = 28 d). Following erdafitinib administration, PK samples for erdafitinib were collected for over 336 h. As seen in **Table 60**, erdafitinib AUC and C_{max} were similar when administered with or without a high-fat, high-calorie meal. Median T_{max} slightly increased from 2.5 to 4.0 h with the meal.
L702: Since no clinically relevant changes in erdafitinib PK were observed, erdafitinib may be taken without regard to food.
L703: **Table 60: Summary of Erdafitinib PK Results from Food Effect Study (EDI1006)**

L707: ## *Drug-Drug Interaction (in vivo and model-based approaches)*
L708: ## Effects of fluconazole (moderate CYP2C9/moderate CYP3A inhibitor) and itraconazole (strong CYP3A/P-gp inhibitor) on erdafitinib PK
L709: Erdafitinib is metabolized by CYP2C9 and CYP3A. It is a substrate for P-gp. Study EDI1007 was a randomized, open-label, parallel-group, single-center study to evaluate the effects of concomitant fluconazole and itraconazole on single-dose erdafitinib in healthy subjects (n=54). The study used a parallel-group design to avoid a long washout period between dosing. Subjects were randomized (1:1:1) by CYP2C9 genotype into 1 of the following 3 dosing groups:
L710: Dosing Group (n)	CYP2C9 Polymorphism (n)	Dosing
*1/*1	*1/*2	*1/*3	Victim (Erdafitinib)	Perpetrator
A (n=19)	12	5	2	Day 1:
4 mg erdafitinib SD	none
B (n=18)	12	4	2	Day 5:
4 mg erdafitinib SD	Days 1-11:
400 mg fluconazole QD
C (n=17)	13	4	0	Day 5:
4 mg erdafitinib SD	Days 1-11:
200 mg itraconazole QD
L711: SD = single-dose, QD = once daily
L712: Following erdafitinib administration under fasted condition, PK samples were collected from pre-dose to post-dose 28 day. An exploratory objective of this study was to explore the effect of CYP2C9 genotype on erdafitinib PK. However, the protocol excluded subjects with CYP2C9 poor metabolizer genotypes (CYP2C9*2/*2, *2/*3, *3/*3). Additionally, the protocol did not enroll subjects with CYP2C9*1/*3 genotypes in Dosing Group C due to potential safety concerns regarding inhibition of erdafitinib metabolic pathways. As seen in **Table 61**, for the primary analysis, concomitant fluconazole increased erdafitinib AUC by 48% and C_{max} by 21%, while concomitant itraconazole increased erdafitinib AUC by 34% but did not significantly change C_{max}. Based on the observed erdafitinib exposure increases, for concomitant strong CYP2C9 / CYP3A inhibitors, patients should consider alternative agents or monitor closely for adverse reactions.
